Recommended route: Fill the worksheet in this app, step by step. It saves as you type, on this device first and then online, and you can download a copy at any time. Alternative route: Prefer a file on your computer? Use the local text-file route below with the copyable starter; then note the file location in Your work.

- Write answers in this course. Keep drawings in your own paper folder or file and record their location. You can stop and resume after any action.


### Order and index

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-brief.

The record ordered by what happened, with an index, and dates, counts and roles rather than names.

- Arrange artefacts in the order they happened.
- Write an index naming what each part contains.

**Start here:** List the artefacts in the order they were made before arranging anything.

**Enough:** Somebody could follow the project from the index alone.

**Ordered by what happened:** Chronological rather than arranged for effect. The order is itself evidence that the process ran in the order you say it did.

**People by role:** The owner, a customer, a reviewer. It is what the consent introduction promised and it reads better than initials.


### The record in order, with an index

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: write-ordered-index.

By what happened rather than by what looks best. The order is itself evidence that the process ran.

**Answer:** The record in order, with an index

By what happened rather than by what looks best. The order is itself evidence that the process ran.


### Dates, counts and people by role rather than by name

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: write-dates-counts.

Write your answer for “Dates, counts and people by role rather than by name”. Use the task instructions below to decide what to include.

**Answer:** Dates, counts and people by role rather than by name




### Each item’s evidence tier: real participant research, self-pilot, supplied practice or simulation

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: write-tier-labels.

The label travels into the portfolio. A practice item or a demonstration with made-up records is never presented as research or real use.

**Answer:** Each item’s evidence tier: real participant research, self-pilot, supplied practice or simulation

The label travels into the portfolio. A practice item or a demonstration with made-up records is never presented as research or real use.


### Check the claims

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-2-brief.

Each claim matched to the artefact behind it, with anything unsupported removed or weakened.

- List every assertion the record makes.
- Attach an artefact, weaken it, or delete it.

**Start here:** Go claim by claim and write the file name beside each.

**Enough:** Every claim has something you could open.

**An artefact behind a claim:** A session record, a tally sheet, a version of the file. A claim with nothing behind it is a sentence you will not be able to defend in an interview.

**Weakening:** Making the claim smaller so the evidence covers it. It is nearly always available and nearly always better than removal.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-2-demo.

Made-up example. Checking claims against artefacts, and finding one with nothing behind it.

**What the record said:** That the page reduced interruptions for the shop. A reasonable-sounding summary of eleven calls becoming seven.

**What the artefact behind it was:** Two tally sheets, five working days each, counting calls about progress before and after the slip wording changed.

**What the claim added:** Two things the sheets do not cover: the page, which stayed a demonstration and was never in real use, and interruptions, which include deliveries, people arriving and every other call.

**What was left when I checked:** A claim about a thing nobody used and a category nobody counted, drawn from a real count of something narrower.

**What I wrote instead:** After the slip wording changed, calls asking about progress went from eleven to seven over matched five-day periods, with the conditions. Smaller, exact, and covered by the sheets.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is summarising a specific measurement into a general category, because the general version reads better and feels like the same thing. The artefact covers the narrow claim and nothing else.

**Trade-off:** The exact version needs its conditions attached every time it is quoted, which makes it clumsy in a sentence.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: whether total interruptions fell. Quite possibly, and nothing counted them, and the record now says which of the two was measured.


### Each claim in the record, with the artefact behind it

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: write-claim-artefact.

Write your answer for “Each claim in the record, with the artefact behind it”. Use the task instructions below to decide what to include.

**Answer:** Each claim in the record, with the artefact behind it




### Any claim with nothing behind it, removed or weakened

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: write-unsupported.

Write your answer for “Any claim with nothing behind it, removed or weakened”. Use the task instructions below to decide what to include.

**Answer:** Any claim with nothing behind it, removed or weakened




### Keep the failures

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-brief.

The failures kept, with why each is load-bearing.

- Check that failed recruitment, repairs and measurements are present.
- Restore anything you were tempted to leave out.

**Start here:** Find the result you were tempted to leave out, and put it back first.

**Enough:** At least one thing in the record went wrong.

**Load-bearing failure:** One the record needs: the repair that did not work, the deviation, the check not run. Without them the successes read as selection.

**Tidying:** Removing what went wrong to make the project read better. It removes the evidence that anything was tested at all.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-demo.

Made-up example. Assembling a project record, and tidying it for the reader.

**What I removed:** The re-test where one person still read the time as a promise. The repair had mostly worked, and the failure made the ending untidy.

**Why it seemed reasonable:** The record was long, the point was made, and the successful repair was the finding. Nothing about it felt dishonest at the time.

**What the record then said:** That I found a problem, changed one thing, and it worked. Which is what every project record says, and which is why nobody believes them.

**What putting it back did:** One of two people still described it as a promise. The wording is better and not solved, and the record now shows a repair being tested rather than a repair being announced.

**What a reviewer said about it:** That it was the first thing they believed. A project where one repair half-worked is a project where somebody actually looked.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is removing the untidy result, because the record is long and the point is already made. What goes with it is the evidence that anything was tested, and every claim afterwards is read as selection.

**Trade-off:** Keeping the failure means the ending is inconclusive, and somebody skimming will see an unresolved problem.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: whether a third version would have fixed it. The appetite ran out, and the record says that rather than implying the problem was solved.


### The failures kept in the record, and why each is load-bearing

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: write-failures-kept.

The repair that did not work, the deviation, the check not run. They are what make the successes believable.

**Answer:** The failures kept in the record, and why each is load-bearing

The repair that did not work, the deviation, the check not run. They are what make the successes believable.


### Anonymise

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-brief.

Everything anonymised to match what the consent introduction promised.

- Replace names with roles throughout.
- Separate consent records from anything shareable.

**Start here:** Read the consent introduction first, then the record.

**Enough:** Nothing in the record breaks what you told people.

**Anonymising:** Roles rather than names, no identifiable property, no real job numbers. It is a promise you already made rather than a precaution.

**Matching the consent:** Checking the record against what you told people would happen. It is the only document that can tell you whether you kept your word.


### Try the distinction · 1 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-sort-1.

Six items from a made up project record. For each one, decide whether it can be included as it is.

A photograph of the tally sheet, showing pencil marks and nothing else.

- include as it is
- include after changing something
- do not include

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

include as it is — It identifies nobody and it is the evidence behind the count. This is the strongest kind of artefact.

include after changing something — Nothing on it needs changing.

do not include — It is what makes the eleven-to-seven claim checkable.

Now check your own record the same way, against what you promised people.

</details>


### Try the distinction · 2 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-sort-2.

Six items from a made up project record. For each one, decide whether it can be included as it is.

A photograph of the workbench showing the job-tracking sheet, with three other customers’ tickets in the frame.

- include as it is
- include after changing something
- do not include

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

include as it is — Three people who consented to nothing have their names and jobs in it.

include after changing something — Cropping might work, and the consent introduction said no photographs including other customers’ items. The promise was not conditional on cropping.

do not include — Redraw the sheet by hand if the record needs it.

Now check your own record the same way, against what you promised people.

</details>


### Try the distinction · 3 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-sort-3.

Six items from a made up project record. For each one, decide whether it can be included as it is.

A quotation from the owner, attributed as “the shop owner”.

- include as it is
- include after changing something
- do not include

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

include as it is — A role rather than a name, which is what the consent introduction promised.

include after changing something — It is already in the agreed form.

do not include — His words are central to the framing.

Now check your own record the same way, against what you promised people.

</details>


### Try the distinction · 4 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-sort-4.

Six items from a made up project record. For each one, decide whether it can be included as it is.

A photograph of a real job slip showing a customer’s name and phone number beside the new window wording.

- include as it is
- include after changing something
- do not include

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

include as it is — The name and number belong to a real person who did not agree to appear anywhere.

include after changing something — Recreate the slip with invented details and label it recreated, then delete the original photograph. The wording is worth showing and the customer’s details are not.

do not include — A recreated slip shows the wording perfectly well, so the evidence need not be lost.

Now check your own record the same way, against what you promised people.

</details>


### Try the distinction · 5 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-sort-5.

Six items from a made up project record. For each one, decide whether it can be included as it is.

The record of the interview the owner’s son sat in on, including his remarks.

- include as it is
- include after changing something
- do not include

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

include as it is — He was present rather than a participant, and nobody took him through the consent introduction.

include after changing something — Keep the interview, remove his remarks, and record that a third party was present and that it changed what was said, without quoting him. The deviation is the finding; his words are not yours to use.

do not include — The owner consented to the interview, and the deviation itself matters and should be recorded.

Now check your own record the same way, against what you promised people.

</details>


### Try the distinction · 6 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-sort-6.

Six items from a made up project record. For each one, decide whether it can be included as it is.

The re-test where one of two people still read the time as a promise.

- include as it is
- include after changing something
- do not include

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

include as it is — Load-bearing. Without it the record says a problem was found, changed and solved, which is what every record says.

include after changing something — Nothing needs softening.

do not include — Removing it removes the evidence that anything was tested.

Now check your own record the same way, against what you promised people.

</details>

### Check your reasoning · 1 of 3

Section: check. Stable action: reason-1.

Choose the reason you believe, read the feedback, then improve the relevant answer if needed.

How should the project record be ordered?

- By what is most impressive, so that a reader meets the best work first.
- By what happened, because the order itself shows the process ran as you say.
- By artefact type, so every session note and file is easy to find.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

By what is most impressive, so that a reader meets the best work first. — That is the case study’s job, in the next module, from this material.

By what happened, because the order itself shows the process ran as you say. — A record arranged for effect puts the outcome first and the research after it, which is the shape of a case study rather than a record. Module 19 makes the case study from this.

By artefact type, so every session note and file is easy to find. — Findable, and it loses the sequence, which is the part that shows the process.

Improve: Order the record chronologically in step 1 and add an index. Record the change in step 5.

Check again: Somebody could follow the project from the index alone.

Answers to revisit: ordered-index, dates-counts, tier-labels, improvement-made

</details>


### Check your reasoning · 2 of 3

Section: check. Stable action: reason-2.

Choose the reason you believe, read the feedback, then improve the relevant answer if needed.

You are tempted to leave out the re-test where the repair only half worked. What would that cost?

- The evidence that anything was tested; the record would read like every other record.
- Some completeness, which a long record can afford to lose for the sake of clarity.
- Very little, since the repair mostly worked and one of two is a small number.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

The evidence that anything was tested; the record would read like every other record. — A project where one repair half-worked is a project where somebody actually looked. Reviewers find it more credible than a clean ending, because clean endings are what selection produces.

Some completeness, which a long record can afford to lose for the sake of clarity. — The failures are the load-bearing part rather than the padding.

Very little, since the repair mostly worked and one of two is a small number. — Mostly is the word doing the work, and the count is what makes it checkable.

Improve: Put the failures back in step 3 and say why each is load-bearing. Record the change in step 5.

Check again: At least one thing in your record went wrong.

Answers to revisit: failures-kept, improvement-made

</details>

### Your decision for the new case, and why

Section: practice. Stable action: write-transfer-decision.

Write your decision first, then the reason it fits this new case. Compare with the example answers only after writing.

**New case.** Made-up case: Your project record for a bakery’s pre-order form contains: “the form halved wasted bakes” (backed by nothing), a tally showing 8 then 5 unsold custom cakes over two matched weeks, a photograph of the order book with customers’ names on it, and no mention of the test session where the form failed for one person.

**Task:** What do you keep, change, cut or restore before the record is complete? Give a reason for each.

<details>
<summary>Compare after writing</summary>

- Weak: Keeps the halving claim because the tally roughly agrees, includes the order-book photograph, and leaves the failed session out to keep the ending tidy.
- Adequate: Cuts or weakens the halving claim to “8 then 5 over matched weeks”, recreates the order book with invented names, and restores the failed session as load-bearing evidence.
- Strong: As adequate, and adds the conditions behind the counts, labels each item’s evidence tier, and stores the record in two places with consent records kept apart.

</details>

**Answer:** Your decision for the new case, and why

<details>
<summary>Optional: learn with an AI app</summary>

Optional learning activity: use a text-based AI chat to hear the idea another way and practise it through questions.

- Open any text-based AI chat you already use. A free option is enough; do not start a trial or upgrade for this activity.
- Start a new chat, copy the whole prompt below and paste it into the message box. Then send it.
- Answer in your own words. Do not paste names, account details, private participant notes or confidential work. Stop after the short activity and return to the named course answer.

```text
I am a complete beginner learning product design. Teach me through a short activity, not a long lecture.

Lesson: Assembling the project record
What I am trying to do: Assemble every artefact from this project into an ordered record that someone else could follow, and check that each claim in it is supported.

Key idea or terms:
Ordered by what happened: Chronological rather than arranged for effect. The order is itself evidence that the process ran in the order you say it did.
People by role: The owner, a customer, a reviewer. It is what the consent introduction promised and it reads better than initials.
An artefact behind a claim: A session record, a tally sheet, a version of the file. A claim with nothing behind it is a sentence you will not be able to defend in an interview.

Supplied practice material (fictional or labelled practice, not my research):
Made-up example. Checking claims against artefacts, and finding one with nothing behind it. That the page reduced interruptions for the shop. A reasonable-sounding summary of eleven calls becoming seven.

Activity: Act as a project reviewer using only the supplied case. Ask for my decision, the evidence behind it and the gap I would investigate next. Do not invent users, results or impact.

Ask one question at a time, at most three questions. Give a small hint only if I ask; leave the decisions and revision to me. Use only the anonymized material I paste. Label role-play as simulation. Never invent participants, quotes, research results or measured impact. Do not award a score or pass. If evidence is missing, say what is missing. Finish by asking me to revise one part and explain why.
When the activity is finished, tell me to return to the course answer called “The record in order, with an index” and write my own decision. Do not write that answer for me.
```

**Come back to the course:** Return to “The record in order, with an index”. Write or revise the answer in your own words, then name one reason for your choice. The AI conversation is practice; your course answer is the work you keep.

<summary>Optional hints and reference material</summary>

- Put the artefacts in the order they happened and write the index.
- List the assertions and resolve each against the artefacts.

- R59: [GOV.UK: sharing user research findings](https://www.gov.uk/service-manual/user-research/sharing-user-research-findings) — Formats for sharing findings, from show and tells to a research wall. Purpose: Shapes how the project record is assembled and stored. Free reading, no account. Verified 2026-09-06. Assumes shared team storage; adapt to your own two locations. Fallback: R04.

Purpose: Keeps consent material out of anything shareable. Free reading, no account. Verified 2026-09-06; last updated 5 November 2018. </details>
<details>
<summary>Creator review and remediation criteria</summary>

**The record is chronological with an index**

Adequate evidence: Ordered artefacts and an index naming their contents.

0 — Unordered files.

1 — Ordered without an index.

2 — Ordered with an index.

3 — As adequate, and someone else could follow it without you.

Repair: Put the artefacts in the order they happened and write the index. Recheck: The index.

**Every claim is supported, weakened or removed**

Adequate evidence: A claims list with each item resolved.

0 — No check performed.

1 — Checked informally.

2 — Every claim resolved.

3 — As adequate, and at least one claim was weakened by the check.

Repair: List the assertions and resolve each against the artefacts. Recheck: The claims check.

**Failures are present in the record**

Adequate evidence: Failed recruitment, repairs or measurements retained.

0 — Failures removed.

1 — Some retained.

2 — All retained.

3 — As adequate, and one failure is explained rather than just listed.

Repair: Restore what you were tempted to leave out. Recheck: The record.

**Participants are anonymised and the record is stored twice**

Adequate evidence: Roles instead of names, consent stored separately, two copies.

0 — Names present, one copy.

1 — Anonymised but stored once.

2 — Both done.

3 — As adequate, and the second copy has been verified as readable.

Repair: Replace names with roles and copy the record somewhere else today. Recheck: The stored record.

</details>
The progress bar counts required actions with saved work. It is not a score or proof of mastery. Your answers and exact action save to this device first, then online. Formative answers are saved for return, not scored. In Your work, review all required answers and record the repair or why none was needed, then choose Finish practice. Optional and unavailable-participant fields do not require invented work. Request creator feedback separately. A file reference does not upload the file. The timer records time while you actively use this course. It pauses outside the course and after five quiet minutes; add external work time manually. Time never completes practice.

**Keep for later:** Open Your work and choose Ready for review. This closes Module 18. Module 19 turns your project records — two on the core path, three on the full library — into a portfolio.

**Review criteria:**

- The record is chronological with an index
- Every claim is supported, weakened or removed
- Failures are present in the record
- Participants are anonymised and the record is stored twice

<details>
<summary>Reading, video and deeper explanation</summary>

- Chronological order is the honest order and the easiest to check. A record arranged to build a narrative is already a case study, and case studies written before the record exists tend to acquire claims the record cannot support.
- The claim-to-artefact check is mechanical and worth doing: read each sentence that asserts something, and find the file that supports it. Anything unsupported either gets its artefact attached, gets weakened to what the evidence shows, or gets deleted.
- The failures carry weight. The recruitment that did not work, the repair that did not fix the problem, the measurement confounded by a public holiday, the prototype that failed the stop gate — these are what make the successes believable, and removing them removes the credibility of everything left.
- Anonymity and durability are both practical. People appear by role, consent records stay out of anything shareable, and the whole record lives in at least two places, because losing three months of work to a failed drive is a common and entirely avoidable ending.

[GOV.UK: sharing user research findings](https://www.gov.uk/service-manual/user-research/sharing-user-research-findings).

</details>
